" jsname="a9kxte"><div jsname="qJTHM" class="kFwPee"><article class="nRhiJb-qJTHM" jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="kxO7ab"><section class="nRhiJb-DARUcf"><div class="Wdmc0c nRhiJb-ObfsIf nRhiJb-fmcmS-oXtfBe"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-c5RTEf"></div><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-qWD73c"><h5 class="cHE8Ub Qwf2Db-MnozTc nRhiJb-DbgRPb-qWD73c-cGMI2b"><div class="nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-ibL1re dzoHJ"><div class="aLW4J" track-metadata-module="left rail"><h5 class="qZDXV Qwf2Db-MnozTc Qwf2Db-MnozTc-OWXEXe-MnozTc-wNfPc">Mandiant Services</h5><p class="nRhiJb-cHYyed Mrfygc nRhiJb-BP2Omd-ma6Yeb-KE6vqe nRhiJb-DbgRPb-R6PoUb-cGMI2b">Stop attacks, reduce risk, and advance your security.</p><a class="nRhiJb-LgbsSe  nRhiJb-LgbsSe-OWXEXe-CNusmb-o6Shpd " href="https://cloud.google.com/security/contact/mandiant-consulting?utm_source=cgc-blog&amp;utm_medium=blog&amp;utm_campaign=FY25-Q3-global-GCP33642-website-cs-dgcsm-mandiant-consulting" Mandiant </a></div></div><div class="OYL9D nRhiJb-kR0ZEf-OWXEXe-GV1x9e-OiUrBf" jsname="tx2NYc"><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p>Written by: Corné de Jong</p>
<hr></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Introduction</span><strong style="vertical-align:baseline"> </strong></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mandiant security assessments frequently identify publicly exposed serverless applications that lack authentication, often as a result of specific business requirements. Serverless deployments typically run custom-developed code that incorporates third-party packages, making them targets for a wide range of application-level attacks, including:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Local and Remote File Inclusion (LFI/RFI)</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Command Injection</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Successful exploitation of these vulnerabilities can grant an attacker full control over the underlying container instance. Such access can serve as a foothold that may ultimately lead to a full compromise of the victim’s cloud environment.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Based on lessons learned in customer engagements, in this blog post we describe attack scenarios and provide actionable guidance on how to secure serverless environments. While this analysis focuses on hardening strategies for Google Cloud Run services and functions that must remain publicly accessible, these principles apply universally to any public serverless deployment.</span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">What are Serverless Applications?</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Serverless applications, also described as Function-as-a-Service (FaaS), allow the deployment of individual blocks of code as microservices within a flexible, decoupled, and event-driven cloud architecture without the need to manage underlying infrastructure. These services enable applications and automations to scale automatically and deploy instantly, removing operational overhead. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Serverless services underpin major e-commerce, media, payment processing applications, and AI usage.</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The rapid expansion of generative AI adoption is a significant driver of increased serverless architecture use. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">AI workflows, including chatbot interactions, image generation, “vibe-coding”, and multi-step AI agents rely on serverless functions to complete tasks for users. </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This growth has made securing serverless environments a more pressing challenge for enterprise security teams. </span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Risks of Serverless Application Attacks</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Publicly exposed serverless workloads can serve as an initial access point for threat actors. As noted, these services may contain vulnerabilities within the code, imported packages, or the underlying runtime environment.</span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Once an entry point is exploited, attackers typically attempt to escalate privileges or move laterally. Common techniques observed include:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Extracting secrets stored directly within the application code.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Reviewing application logic and sensitive data to identify further attack vectors within the environment.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Exfiltrating service account bearer tokens from the metadata server following successful Remote Code Execution (RCE).</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Leveraging these compromised secrets or service accounts allows threat actors to pivot to adjacent systems and workloads, potentially resulting in a total environment takeover if proper hardening strategies are not in place.</span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Example Attack Scenarios</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The following simplified scenarios illustrate how serverless functions can be compromised and how attackers pivot after achieving initial code execution.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Local File Inclusion (LFI) </span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In the following Cloud Run example, a Python/Flask function accepts user-controlled input to open a file without performing proper validation. This pattern is an example of a Local File Inclusion (LFI) vulnerability.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>import functions_framework

@functions_framework.http
def hello_http(request):
    request_json = request.get_json(silent=True)
    request_args = request.args
    if request_json and 'file' in request_json:
        file = request_json['file']
    elif request_args and 'file' in request_args:
        file = request_args['file']
 
# VULNERABILITY: The 'file' parameter is used directly in open() 
# without validation, allowing arbitrary file access
    with open(file, 'r') as resp:
          filedata = resp.read()
    return 'local file data {}!'.format(filedata)</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 1: Vulnerable Python/Flask function accepting unvalidated user input to open files</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This vulnerability allows an attacker to request sensitive files from the Cloud Run instance by using </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">curl</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> to send a POST request via the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">file</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> parameter:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>curl -X POST https://cloudrun01-abc.europe-west3.run.app/ -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"file": "main.py"}'</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 2: curl POST request targeting the file parameter</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The response provides the complete </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">main.py</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> source code. An attacker can analyze the code for:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hardcoded secrets such as API keys, database credentials, or authentication tokens</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Business logic flaws and additional injection points</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Internal service endpoints and architecture details</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Import statements revealing the technology stack and potential CVE exposure</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Additionally, attackers can leverage standard </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">../</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> directory traversal sequences to retrieve sensitive system files:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>curl -X POST https://cloudrun01-abc.europe-west3.run.app/ -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d '{"file": "../../../etc/passwd"}'</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 3: curl POST request leveraging directory traversal sequences</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">An LFI vulnerability allows an attacker to retrieve and fuzz various files directly from the container. Key examples include:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">requirements.txt, package.json, go.mod</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Used to identify installed packages and versions with known vulnerabilities.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">env</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> files: Frequently contain sensitive environment variables or hard coded secrets.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Application configuration files: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">May contain database credentials, API keys, or service endpoints if not securely managed.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><code style="vertical-align:baseline">/etc/passwd, /proc/self/environ</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Contains user information, environment variables.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Application logs: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">may contain auth tokens or PII data.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Best Practice:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Never store secrets or credentials within the source code or local container files. Utilize a dedicated secrets management solution, such as Secret Manager.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Code Execution/Command Injection</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">In the following scenario, a Python function uses shell execution methods with unsanitized user input, allowing an attacker to execute arbitrary commands.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>import functions_framework
import subprocess


@functions_framework.http
def hello_http(request):
  request_json = request.get_json(silent=True)
  request_args = request.args
  if request_json and 'input' in request_json:
      input = request_json['input']
  elif request_args and 'input' in request_args:
      input = request_args['input']
  result = subprocess.run(input, shell=True,capture_output=True, text=True)
  return format(result)</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 4: Python function utilizing shell execution with unsanitized user input</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">This allows an attacker to execute a subsequent curl request targeting the GCP metadata service to retrieve the service account’s bearer token. </span></p>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The following request extracts the service account's OAuth 2.0 bearer token, which remains valid for 1 hour:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>curl -X POST https://cloudrun02-abc.europe-west3.run.app/ -H "Content-Type: application/json" -d "{\"input\": \"curl 'http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1/instance/service-accounts/default/token' -H 'Metadata-Flavor: Google'\"}"</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 5:</span><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> </span><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Extraction of a GCP service account bearer token via a curl request</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Once obtained, an attacker can use it on an attacker-controlled system to execute Google Cloud CLI commands. For example the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">CLOUDSDK_AUTH_ACCESS_TOKEN</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> environment variable can be set using the stolen bearer token.</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>export CLOUDSDK_AUTH_ACCESS_TOKEN=”obtain bearer token”</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 6: Defining CLOUDSDK_AUTH_ACCESS_TOKEN environment variable</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Attackers can then leverage Google Cloud Cloud CLI within the security context of the Cloud Run Compute service account. If deployed without best practices and thoughtful configuration controls, for example, if the  Cloud Run service runs as the default compute service account with Editor permissions, this would be equivalent to a full GCP project takeover, and allow the attacker to:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Read/write/delete most GCP resources</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Deploy new services and modify existing configurations</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Access secrets and encryption keys</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Exfiltrate data across all accessible storage systems</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Establish persistent backdoors through new service accounts or SSH keys.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hardening Recommendations</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mandiant recommends that organizations implement parallel approaches for effective serverless security:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC): </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">integrate security scanning, code review, least-privilege IAM into CI/CD pipelines before deployment and integrate continuous security testing; </span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Vibe Coding</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">: Mandiant recommends multi-layered security enforcement for AI-generated code or "vibe coding." Organizations should isolate AI experimentation within dedicated sandbox environments and enforce strict data egress controls to protect production systems and internal data. Furthermore, development environments should be restricted to approved IDEs with human-in-the-loop capabilities, utilizing only verified plugins operating under least privilege to mitigate supply chain vulnerabilities. Finally, organizations must ensure this AI-generated software follows Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC) controls while establishing clear internal guidelines regarding permitted use cases. Comprehensive security fundamentals for vibe coding are documented in detail within the </span><a href="https://www.wiz.io/academy/ai-security/vibe-coding-security" rel="noopener" target="_blank"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Wiz Vibe Coding Security Fundamentals blog</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Compensating Runtime Controls: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Implement the following defense-in-depth measures to limit and contain compromise even when application vulnerabilities exist;</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Segregate Public Services</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Host public-facing Cloud Run services consumed by untrusted external entities in a dedicated, isolated Google Cloud project. This ensures a compromise does not provide an immediate path to critical internal resources. The implementation of this 'Service Project' model is beyond the scope of this post; however, it is documented in detail within the </span><a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/architecture/blueprints/serverless-blueprint"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">secured serverless architecture blueprint</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Identity and Access Management (IAM)</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Mandiant recommends using a custom service account for service authentication rather than the default Compute Engine service account, following the principle of least privilege. Grant only the specific permissions necessary for the Cloud Run function to operate, for example:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Cloud Storage Bucket Access:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> If the service only requires read access to objects from a Cloud Storage bucket, grant the </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">Storage Object Viewer</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">roles/storage.objectViewer</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) role restricted to that specific bucket.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Secret Manager Access:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">  If the service requires access to secrets, grant the</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline"> Secret Manager Secret Accessor</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> (</span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">roles/secretmanager.secretAccessor</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline">) role only to the individual secrets required. For further details on secret access from Cloud Run, refer to the </span><a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/run/docs/configuring/services/secrets#required_roles"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">GCP documentation on configuring secrets</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Layer 7 Application Load Balancer (ALB) Architecture</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Restrict ingress traffic for serverless functions to internal only and use an external Layer 7 ALB to manage internet exposure. This provides:</span></p>
<ul>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Centralized Traffic Management:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Granular control over headers and SSL policies.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Cloud Armor Integration:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Web Application Firewall (WAF) support to harden applications against vulnerabilities such as Local/Remote File Inclusion (LFI/RFI) and Server-Side Request Forgery (SSRF).</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Traffic Shaping: </strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Implementation of rate limits and request limitations to prevent abuse.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Enhanced Visibility:</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> Robust logging and log-forwarding capabilities for security monitoring.</span></p>
</li>
<li style="list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline">
<p><strong style="vertical-align:baseline">Identity-Aware Proxy (IAP):</strong><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> integration support for scenarios requiring specific identity-based authentication for internal users.</span></p>
</li>
</ul>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Web Application Firewall (WAF) <span style="vertical-align:baseline">—</span> Cloud Armor</span></h4>
<p><a href="https://cloud.google.com/security/products/armor"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Cloud Armor</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> provides WAF protections that can be integrated with the Load Balancer to filter malicious traffic. The following examples demonstrate how to configure Cloud Armor security policies to block the specific local file inclusions, remote code execution and traversal attacks previously outlined.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Local File Inclusion</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">lfi-v33-stable</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> preconfigured WAF rules can block common local file inclusion attacks (</span><a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/armor/docs/waf-rules#local_file_inclusion_lfi"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">local file inclusion reference</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>evaluatePreconfiguredWaf('lfi-v33-stable', {'sensitivity': 3})</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 7: Cloud Armor lfi-v33-stable WAF rule configuration</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Blocking a path traversal request </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">../../../etc/passwd</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> resulting in a 403 forbidden:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>curl -X POST https://exampleabc01.com -H &quot;Content-Type: application/json&quot; -d &#39;{&quot;file&quot;: &quot;../../../etc/passwd}&#39;
&lt;!doctype html&gt;&lt;meta charset=&quot;utf-8&quot;&gt;&lt;meta name=viewport content=&quot;width=device-width, initial-scale=1&quot;&gt;&lt;title&gt;403&lt;/title&gt;403 Forbidden</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 8: Verification of Cloud Armor blocking path traversal request, resulting in a 403 forbidden</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Remote Code Execution</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">The </span><code style="vertical-align:baseline">rce-v33-stable</code><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> preconfigured WAF rules can block remote code execution attempts (</span><a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/armor/docs/waf-rules#remote_code_execution_rce"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">remote code execution reference</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline">).</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>evaluatePreconfiguredWaf('rce-v33-stable', {'sensitivity': 3})</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 9: Cloud Armor rce-v33-stable WAF rule configuration</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Blocking the remote code execution request from the previous example results in a 403 forbidden:</span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><pre><code>curl -X POST https://exampleabc01.com -H &quot;Contencurl -X POST https://exampleabc01.com -H &quot;Content-Type: application/json&quot; -d &quot;{\&quot;input\&quot;: \&quot;curl &#39;http://metadata.google.internal/computeMetadata/v1/instance/service-accounts/default/token&#39; -H &#39;Metadata-Flavor: Google&#39;\&quot;}&quot;
&lt;!doctype html&gt;&lt;meta charset=&quot;utf-8&quot;&gt;&lt;meta name=viewport content=&quot;width=device-width, initial-scale=1&quot;&gt;&lt;title&gt;403&lt;/title&gt;403 Forbidden</code></pre>
<p style="text-align:center"><span style="color:#5f6368;display:block;font-size:16px;font-style:italic;margin-top:8px;width:100%"><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Figure 10: Verification of Cloud Armor blocking Remote Code execution, resulting in a 403 forbidden</span></span></p></div></section><section class="Wy08Ac nRhiJb-qJTHM-OWXEXe-hJDwNd nRhiJb-DbgRPb-II5mzb-cGMI2b"><div jsaction="rcuQ6b:npT2md" jscontroller="M0Q3Qb" data-is-cws-richtext="true" class=" "><h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Serverless Architecture Controls</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Hardening Cloud Run services is only one part of a secure architecture. Because these services often connect to other Google Cloud resources, a single compromise can expose additional services. Implementing defense-in-depth is critical. Specifically, when using direct VPC egress or VPC Access connectors, use VPC Service Controls to restrict lateral movement and exfiltration through granular access policies.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC)</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">While the previously outlined hardening strategies are critical, the ideal standard remains the proactive identification of vulnerabilities during the initial development stages. A deep dive into "Shift-Left" security is beyond the scope of this analysis, which focuses on mitigating risks within existing code. However, a Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC) remains a fundamental principle. Robust code validation and continuous security testing are essential to neutralize threats before serverless functions are published externally.</span></p>
<h4><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Cloud Run Threat Detection</span></h4>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Beyond the hardening recommendations outlined in this post, </span><a href="https://cloud.google.com/security/products/security-command-center"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Google Cloud Security Command Center (SCC)</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> provides built-in services to detect control plane attacks against Cloud Run resources. These include detectors for credential access, reconnaissance, and the execution of scripts or reverse shells. The </span><a href="https://docs.cloud.google.com/security-command-center/docs/cloud-run-threat-detection-overview"><span style="text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline">Cloud Run Threat Detection</span></a><span style="vertical-align:baseline"> service is available for Premium and Enterprise tiers.</span></p>
<h3><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Conclusion</span></h3>
<p><span style="vertical-align:baseline">Serverless applications drive agility and rapid business value. While "vibe-coding" has made it easier than ever to deploy code, this breakneck speed demands that teams integrate security early in the development lifecycle, move beyond default configurations, and prioritize a defense-in-depth strategy centered on identity and architecture. </span></p>

style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMandiant security assessments frequently identify publicly exposed serverless applications that lack authentication, often as a result of specific business requirements. Serverless deployments typically run custom-developed code that incorporates third-party packages, making them targets for a wide range of application-level attacks, including:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLocal and Remote File Inclusion (LFI/RFI)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCommand Injection\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSuccessful exploitation of these vulnerabilities can grant an attacker full control over the underlying container instance. Such access can serve as a foothold that may ultimately lead to a full compromise of the victim’s cloud environment.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBased on lessons learned in customer engagements, in this blog post we describe attack scenarios and provide actionable guidance on how to secure serverless environments. While this analysis focuses on hardening strategies for Google Cloud Run services and functions that must remain publicly accessible, these principles apply universally to any public serverless deployment.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWhat are Serverless Applications?\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eServerless applications, also described as Function-as-a-Service (FaaS), allow the deployment of individual blocks of code as microservices within a flexible, decoupled, and event-driven cloud architecture without the need to manage underlying infrastructure. These services enable applications and automations to scale automatically and deploy instantly, removing operational overhead. \u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eServerless services underpin major e-commerce, media, payment processing applications, and AI usage.\u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThe rapid expansion of generative AI adoption is a significant driver of increased serverless architecture use. \u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eAI workflows, including chatbot interactions, image generation, “vibe-coding”, and multi-step AI agents rely on serverless functions to complete tasks for users. \u003c/span\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eThis growth has made securing serverless environments a more pressing challenge for enterprise security teams. \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRisks of Serverless Application Attacks\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003ePublicly exposed serverless workloads can serve as an initial access point for threat actors. As noted, these services may contain vulnerabilities within the code, imported packages, or the underlying runtime environment.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eOnce an entry point is exploited, attackers typically attempt to escalate privileges or move laterally. Common techniques observed include:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eExtracting secrets stored directly within the application code.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eReviewing application logic and sensitive data to identify further attack vectors within the environment.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eExfiltrating service account bearer tokens from the metadata server following successful Remote Code Execution (RCE).\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLeveraging these compromised secrets or service accounts allows threat actors to pivot to adjacent systems and workloads, potentially resulting in a total environment takeover if proper hardening strategies are not in place.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eExample Attack Scenarios\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan execution.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLocal File Inclusion (LFI) \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIn the following Cloud Run example, a Python/Flask function accepts user-controlled input to open a file without performing proper validation. Recommendations\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMandiant recommends that organizations implement parallel approaches for effective serverless security:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSecure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC): \u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eintegrate security scanning, code review, least-privilege IAM into CI/CD pipelines before deployment and integrate continuous security testing; \u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eVibe Coding\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e: Mandiant recommends multi-layered security enforcement for AI-generated code or \"vibe coding.\" Organizations should isolate AI experimentation within dedicated sandbox environments and enforce strict data egress controls to protect production systems and internal data. Furthermore, development environments should be restricted to approved IDEs with human-in-the-loop capabilities, utilizing only verified plugins operating under least privilege to mitigate supply chain vulnerabilities. Finally, organizations must ensure this AI-generated software follows Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC) controls while establishing clear internal guidelines regarding permitted use cases. Comprehensive security fundamentals for vibe coding are documented in detail within the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://www.wiz.io/academy/ai-security/vibe-coding-security\" rel\u003d\"noopener\" target\u003d\"_blank\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWiz Vibe Coding Security Fundamentals blog\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCompensating Runtime Controls: \u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eImplement the following defense-in-depth measures to limit and contain compromise even when application vulnerabilities exist;\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSegregate Public Services\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eHost public-facing Cloud Run services consumed by untrusted external entities in a dedicated, isolated Google Cloud project. This ensures a compromise does not provide an immediate path to critical internal resources. The implementation of this 'Service Project' model is beyond the scope of this post; however, it is documented in detail within the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.cloud.google.com/architecture/blueprints/serverless-blueprint\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003esecured serverless architecture blueprint\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eIdentity and Access Management (IAM)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eMandiant recommends using a custom service account for service authentication rather than the default Compute Engine service account, following the principle of least privilege. Grant only the specific permissions necessary for the Cloud Run function to operate, for example:\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCloud Storage Bucket Access:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e If the service only requires read access to objects from a Cloud Storage bucket, grant the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eStorage Object Viewer\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eroles/storage.objectViewer\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) role restricted to that specific bucket.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli style\u003d\"list-style-type:disc;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSecret Manager Access:\u003c/strong\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e  If the service requires access to secrets, grant the\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e Secret Manager Secret Accessor\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e (\u003c/span\u003e\u003ccode style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eroles/secretmanager.secretAccessor\u003c/code\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e) role only to the individual secrets required. For further details on secret access from Cloud Run, refer to the \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.cloud.google.com/run/docs/configuring/services/secrets#required_roles\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGCP documentation on configuring secrets\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eLayer 7 Application Load Balancer (ALB) Architecture\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eRestrict ingress traffic for serverless functions to internal only and use an external Layer 7 ALB to manage internet exposure. Controls\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eHardening Cloud Run services is only one part of a secure architecture. Because these services often connect to other Google Cloud resources, a single compromise can expose additional services. Implementing defense-in-depth is critical. Specifically, when using direct VPC egress or VPC Access connectors, use VPC Service Controls to restrict lateral movement and exfiltration through granular access policies.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eSecure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eWhile the previously outlined hardening strategies are critical, the ideal standard remains the proactive identification of vulnerabilities during the initial development stages. A deep dive into \"Shift-Left\" security is beyond the scope of this analysis, which focuses on mitigating risks within existing code. However, a Secure Software Development Lifecycle (S-SDLC) remains a fundamental principle. Robust code validation and continuous security testing are essential to neutralize threats before serverless functions are published externally.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch4\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCloud Run Threat Detection\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h4\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eBeyond the hardening recommendations outlined in this post, \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://cloud.google.com/security/products/security-command-center\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eGoogle Cloud Security Command Center (SCC)\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e provides built-in services to detect control plane attacks against Cloud Run resources. These include detectors for credential access, reconnaissance, and the execution of scripts or reverse shells. The \u003c/span\u003e\u003ca href\u003d\"https://docs.cloud.google.com/security-command-center/docs/cloud-run-threat-detection-overview\"\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"text-decoration:underline;vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eCloud Run Threat Detection\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003e service is available for Premium and Enterprise tiers.\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eConclusion\u003c/span\u003e\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cspan style\u003d\"vertical-align:baseline\"\u003eServerless applications drive agility and rapid business value. While \"vibe-coding\" has made it easier than ever to deploy code, this breakneck speed demands that teams integrate security early in the development lifecycle, move beyond default configurations,
